Welcome back to World Brief, where we’re looking at long-anticipated U.S.-China talks , Israel ’s and the Palestinian Authority ’s U.N. speeches, and the risk of all-out war in Ethiopia . It’s been more than a decade since Chinese President Xi Jinping visited the United States, and in that time, geopolitical tensions between the superpowers have only heightened. Artificial intelligence, critical minerals, human rights, tariffs, and Taiwan were just a few of the items on U.S. President Donald Trump’s agenda as he welcomed Xi to the White House on Thursday. But a day that began with much pomp and pageantry suffered from little substance and concluded with few major breakthroughs—that is, unless you count Xi’s announcement that two giant pandas will be sent to the Atlanta Zoo. Thursday’s high-level summit was in the works for months, though the White House’s preparation was “unsurprisingly chaotic and incompetent,” FP’s James Palmer wrote this week. Still, in a sign of goodwill, both leaders issued statements on Thursday appealing to the need for closer cooperation . “Achieving the great rejuvenation of the Chinese nation and making America great again can go hand in hand,” Xi told reporters, with Trump similarly hailing his “truly great friendship” with Xi and even greeting the Chinese leader on the tarmac rather than at the White House. That is where the leaders’ shared interests appeared to end. At time of writing, Trump and Xi have not announced any comprehensive deals addressing tariff concerns, rare-earths competition, or soybean purchases. Late on Wednesday, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent agreed to extend the countries’ trade truce by another two months; the deal was set to expire in mid-November. * Saudi Arabia Bribed the Wrong People
  Foreign Policy | 24 Sep 17:36 UTC
  Riyadh’s bet on Trumpism has failed.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Saudi Arabia is in serious trouble. Its global influence primarily comes from its leading position as an oil producer and the money resulting from that role, but now the Middle East is on fire. Today, Saudi tankers are blocked in the Persian Gulf and under threat in the Red Sea, and the country’s biggest oil pipeline had to shut down after a drone attack . Yet Saudi Arabia’s biggest long-term problem may be that its longtime security guarantor, the United States, is unable to put out the fire and doesn’t seem especially interested in trying. Since World War II and especially since the end of the Cold War, the Middle East has rested on a deal: U.S. military dominance provides relative stability, deterring and countering aggression, ensuring freedom of navigation, containing Iran, organizing air defense, and helping friendly governments to stay in power. In exchange, Saudi Arabia and the smaller Gulf Arab states—Kuwait, Bahrain, Qatar, and the United Arab Emirates—host U.S. bases, cooperate on counterterrorism, and maintain the smooth flow of oil. The clearest example of this working was in 1990-91, when a U.S.-led coalition prevented Iraq from taking any Saudi oil fields and pushed Iraqi forces out of Kuwait. * Can AI Be Regulated?
  Foreign Policy | 24 Sep 16:14 UTC
  Frontier AI labs are calling for government oversight.
  FULL TEXT: Momentum has been building for the U.S. federal government to impose regulations on artificial intelligence. U.S. President Donald Trump opposes new strictures on the industry, but the public has grown concerned about the potential for the technology to produce catastrophic consequences for the economy and human society more broadly. Might normal liability law be adequate for regulating AI technology? How does the apocalyptic potential of AI relate to the financial value of AI companies? Does the U.S. government have sufficient expertise to regulate AI? Momentum has been building for the U.S. federal government to impose regulations on artificial intelligence. U.S. President Donald Trump opposes new strictures on the industry, but the public has grown concerned about the potential for the technology to produce catastrophic consequences for the economy and human society more broadly. Might normal liability law be adequate for regulating AI technology? How does the apocalyptic potential of AI relate to the financial value of AI companies? Does the U.S. government have sufficient expertise to regulate AI? Those are just a few of the questions that came up in my recent conversation with FP economics columnist Adam Tooze on the podcast we co-host, Ones and Tooze . What follows is an excerpt, edited for length and clarity. For the full conversation, look for Ones and Tooze wherever you get your podcasts. And check out Adam’s Substack newsletter. Cameron Abadi: In what ways could we expect liability law and criminal law to mitigate the dangers of AI? Adam Tooze: Broadly speaking, this could be done probably under negligence, where an actor fails to anticipate foreseeable risk. Product liability could do this, or maybe nuisance. Professional malpractice, contract and consumer protection law—there’s lots of different angles you could go down. And the full ambiguity of this is brought out by the fact that, after the top guys at OpenAI and Anthropic came out with these apocalyptic warnings and now they want to pace the frontier, not only did the Trump administration dissent, but [CEO] Jensen Huang of Nvidia and Mark Zuckerberg of Meta also dissented from this position. * The Law of the Sea Is in Trouble
  Foreign Policy | 24 Sep 14:38 UTC
  The far right wants to abandon stranded seafarers.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. But these days, some Brits are getting upset with the rescuers—to the point of attacking them or those they’re saving—because many of the people that they’re trying to save are attempting to enter the United Kingdom illegally. There has long been an obligation, moral and legal, to save the lives of those at risk at sea; today, the RNLI upholds that while the U.S. military violates it. In the waters off the British coast, volunteers with the Royal National Lifeboat Institution (RNLI) are doing what they’ve always done: rescue people in trouble. But these days, some Brits are getting upset with the rescuers—to the point of attacking them or those they’re saving—because many of the people that they’re trying to save are attempting to enter the United Kingdom illegally. In the Caribbean and the Pacific, the U.S. military has attacked numerous so-called drug boats. There has long been an obligation, moral and legal, to save the lives of those at risk at sea; today, the RNLI upholds that while the U.S. military violates it. A sense of community between seafarers dates back centuries, but the modern obligation was formalized early last century. Individual nations had written a duty to rescue into their laws throughout the 19th century, but the first real international agreement came in Article 11 of the 1910 Brussels Convention on Assistance and Salvage at Sea: “Every master is bound, so far as he can do so without serious danger to his vessel, her crew and passengers, to render assistance to everybody, even though an enemy, found at sea in danger of being lost.” But it was a famous disaster and a heroic rescue that solidified the rule. Just after midnight on April 14, 1912, the telegraph clerk on the RMS Carpathia , a trans-Atlantic passenger ship, was supposed to finish his shift, but before doing so, he sent an update to the RMS Titanic . It was a few minutes into April 15. To his horror, the Titanic telegraphed back that it had struck an iceberg and was sinking. The telegraph clerk—Harold Cottam—immediately alerted his captain, Arthur Henry Rostron. Wasting no time, Rostron turned his ship around and sped more than 50 nautical miles, through icebergs and other per
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/09/24/migrants-britain-royal-national-lifeboat-institution-united-states-trump/

* Germany Is Officially Adrift
  Foreign Policy | 24 Sep 06:54 UTC
  The German chancellor’s power is ebbing at home—and the consequences will be global.
  FULL TEXT: “Disaster.” German Chancellor Friedrich Merz was not mincing words Sunday night when he described the results of the state elections for his center-right Christian Democratic Union (CDU) party. The CDU fell below the 5 percent threshold in the east German state of Mecklenburg-Vorpommern—the first time in postwar German history that the CDU has failed to win representation in a state election. The far-right Alternative for Germany party (AfD) finished first in Mecklenburg-Vorpommern with 38 percent, the second time that it has notched a first-place finish in September after garnering nearly 44 percent of the vote in the east German state of Saxony-Anhalt. Elections in the city-state of Berlin were no less dramatic, with the Left party (the successor to the East German communist party) finishing first, well ahead of Merz’s CDU. That the CDU would lose badly in September was clear for some time, as was the chancellor’s deep unpopularity: In a recent poll , 84 percent of Germans said that they were unhappy with the performance of Merz’s government. * Scientists gave worms magnetic bacteria. They lived 43% longer

* Interactive: What China’s dozens of ‘five-year plans’ say about climate and energy
  Carbon Brief | 24 Sep 14:00 UTC
  Almost three-quarters of the 72 five-year plans published by China this year include policies or targets related to climate change… The post Interactive: What China’s dozens of ‘five-year plans’ say about climate and energy appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: Almost three-quarters of the 72 five-year plans published by China this year include policies or targets related to climate change and energy, according to Carbon Brief analysis. Five-year plans are an important part of China’s policymaking process, signalling key priorities and setting targets for the coming years. The highest-level 15th five-year plan , which covers “economic and social development” for the period 2026-2030, was published in March 2026. The government has subsequently published a number of thematic five-year plans at the national level that are specifically focused on climate and energy. These include plans on renewable energy, responding to climate change and development of its fossil-fuel industries. Furthermore, the large majority of national-level plans that are not directly connected to climate change also include climate policies – albeit at different levels of detail. Carbon Brief has combed through all of the 15th five-year plans issued by central-government ministries that are currently available in public for any mentions of topics related to climate change. The findings, summarised in the interactive tracker below, show that 54 of the 72 plans published so far include climate policies or targets. For each plan, the tracker lists the most important targets and primary goals, as well as other notable policies and a link to the full text, or a summary if the full text is unavailable. Five-year plans are used to guide economic and social development across China. The overarching plan is published in March at the beginning of each five-year cycle. It outlines the most important high-level targets and policies across all issues. After the top-level plan, a series of other five-year plans are then published over a period of months or even years, each focused on individual sectors or themes. In total, Carbon Brief has tracked the 72 plans published to date which cover the 15th five-year plan period, ranging from topics such as the coal industry and electric vehicles through to accounting methods and tourism. It still remains unknown exactly how many plans for this five-year period are yet to be published – and on what topics – although Carbon Brief understands that the key plans relating to climate and energy have now likely been released. The current five-year plan period is viewed as being particularly important for climate action, as it covers the years building up to China’s pledge to peak its carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions “befor
  https://interactive.carbonbrief.org/china-plan-tracker/index.html

* Analysis: EVs are now nine times cheaper than petrol or diesel to drive in the UK
  Carbon Brief | 24 Sep 11:49 UTC
  The latest surge in fossil-fuel prices means it is now up to nine times cheaper in the UK to drive… The post Analysis: EVs are now nine times cheaper than petrol or diesel to drive in the UK appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The latest surge in fossil-fuel prices means it is now up to nine times cheaper in the UK to drive an electric vehicle (EV) than a petrol or diesel car, shows Carbon Brief analysis. Since February, when the US first attacked Iran , the average price of diesel has increased by 38% to £1.96 a litre, according to Carbon Brief analysis of government figures. Following the attack on the Kapotnya oil refinery in Russia on 20 September and with tensions in the Middle East growing, this is expected to pass a record £2 a litre. (Russia was the second-largest exporter of diesel in the world, but its refineries have been hit every three days on average in the first six months of 2026.) Similarly, petrol prices have surged by 31% since February to £1.72, based on government figures, with some locations reaching nearly £2 per litre, according to BBC News . These increases have been driven by oil prices jumping to more than $100 per barrel as the conflict between Yemen’s Houthis and Saudi Arabia escalates – a roughly 50% increase from June. As such, it now costs an estimated 21p per mile to drive a diesel car and 20.1p per mile for petrol, according to Carbon Brief analysis of the latest figures from the Department of Energy Security and Net Zero (DESNZ). In contrast, it is currently nine times cheaper to drive an EV charged using an off-peak tariff , at 2.3p per mile, as shown in the chart below. Even if charging with electricity bought at the domestic price cap – the maximum amount a supplier can charge for a unit of energy and standing charge, set by the regulator Ofgem every three months – it still only costs around 7p per mile to drive an EV, three times cheaper than the price for petrol or diesel cars. The price cap is set to increase in October, but almost all of the increase is for gas, with the unit rate of electricity only expected to rise by less than 1% from 26.1p per unit to 26.3p. A larger 20% increase in electricity unit rates under the price cap is expected in January 2027, as higher gas costs filter through to an increase in wholesale electricity prices. Nevertheless, EVs would still remain far cheaper to drive than petrol or diesel cars. UK drivers could save around £80 by charging an EV at home, in comparison with filling up a petrol car at the pump, as shown in the figure below. This is based on comparing the cost of an average full tank of petrol with the cost of enough electricity to drive the same distance. Despite their much higher running costs,
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-evs-are-now-nine-times-cheaper-than-petrol-or-diesel-to-drive-in-the-uk

* In Defense of Egyptian Food
  New Lines | 24 Sep 21:00 UTC
  Egyptian food has long had a bad reputation, but it’s time to reassess what you think you know about mahshy, molokhia in duck stock, koshary — and tamiyya versus falafel. The post In Defense of Egyptian Food appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: The first time I told my wife, who is from Aleppo, that I loved her, I was in the throes of a half-coma prompted by eating unnecessarily large quantities of kebab karaz (small meatballs cooked in a sour cherry and pomegranate molasses reduction) and kibbeh labaniyeh (meat dumplings cooked in a yoghurt stew). It was probably the closest I’ve ever come to accepting my own inherent mortality and the peace that comes with knowing that one can be ready to let go — the belly is saturated and the mind is in a stupor, but there is little you want to do to remedy the situation, and the world will take care of itself when you’re gone. Snooze gently into that good night. I started with this anecdote to ameliorate the impact of my next statement: We don’t talk enough about how great Egyptian cuisine is, and I dare say so as an Egyptian. In the past week, social media has been awash with discussion of the merits and shortfalls of the Mother of the World’s cuisine, with much pearl-clutching that evokes Robert Pattinson’s “somebody get these beggars out of here!” line in “The Odyssey,” caused by the whispered suggestion that humble grain-based Egyptian food might actually hold up favorably in comparison to Levantine aristocratic flamboyance, Lebanese mezzes and Syrian kibbeh. As far as I can tell, the latest salvo of the food wars was prompted by a screenshot of a poll in which Egypt trailed Turkey, Lebanon and Iran on the question of which Middle Eastern country had the best food, and subsequent social media posts about how Egyptian food was subpar. Georgetown Islamic civilization professor Jonathan Brown bucked the trend of bashing Egyptian food that usually ensues, by saying that the past 10 years had seen massive improvement in the country’s food scene, likely due to several factors — Gulf investment in restaurants, the presence of Syrian chefs who had fled the war in their country, etc. I grew up between the Gulf and Egypt in a largely Egyptian milieu of extended family and friends, so my experience of Egyptian food was largely centered around home-cooked meals made by my mom, aunts, grandmothers and other Egyptian women in our social circle, particularly during Ramadan, when iftar invitations were frequent. Many of these meals included variations of Egyptian staple dishes (though not koshary, which we’ll come to later, given its history as a working-class dish, making it inappropriate when you invite people over).   Longreads | 24 Sep 19:00 UTC

  FULL TEXT: Duncan is a longtime friend of Max Daly, and a drug addict caught up in “cuckooing,” where dealers take over people’s homes as drug bases in exchange for product. Daly offers a sympathetic portrait of Duncan’s spiralling life, while opening a window onto a bizarre world where the most vulnerable exploit each other—and sometimes even form bonds. Over the 2000s and 2010s, wave after wave of gangs fanned out from Britain’s drug supply hubs – London, Liverpool and Birmingham – to set up shop in smaller towns and cities across the country. They took over the homes of drug users and young women who had been groomed by older male gang members. As early as 2008 Suffolk constabulary reported that more than a third of Class A drug dealers arrested in Suffolk were under 21, and the majority were from London. (Before this period, most crack and heroin dealers in towns and smaller cities were “user-dealers”, addicted heroin users in their 30s or older, who were not as “professionalised” as the big city drug gangs.) Gangs often used sex workers, who targeted male tenants and moved in, bringing the young dealers with them. Community safety teams, who rescued 80 tenants in London in 2005 , were the first to call the tactic “cuckooing”. “How Jos Leijdekkers turned Sierra Leone into a drug-dealer’s paradise.” “What happens to the disappeared in Mexico’s narco-slavery camps.” “Before Ryan Wedding landed on the FBI’s Most Wanted list as a high-level associate of El Chapo, he was a bright-eyed kid from Thunder Bay. * Heaven’s Melodic Strains
  Longreads | 24 Sep 17:22 UTC
  "From a certain height, the problem becomes one of knowledge itself: Do you believe what you hear, or do you hear what you believe?"
  FULL TEXT: Audiophiles are sometimes described in terms reserved for cults; a 1956 New Yorker story, referenced in Colin Vanderburg’s exceptional tour of extreme listening, refers to “a remarkably articulate group of zealots” and “an almost mystical reverence for sound.” Vanderburg travels among the believers, tracing the decades-long promise of “consumer audio innovations” to their technological apex: the New York International Audio Show, where audiophiles sample six-figure sound systems in pursuit of a signal as pure as the world they already inhabit. More than anything, I have spent long passages of this earthly life sitting on my ass, listening. I listen to music, but what I hear is sound. Texture, timbre, and tone; clarity and detail; space and depth. And I hear, even more acutely, what thwarts sound. Shrill treble, muddled bass — for me these are worse than peeves. They offend hearing. They diminish the human sensorium. Earbuds repulse me; laptop speakers grate; a tabletop Bluetooth unit is a kind of vandalism. The word audiophile — etymologically, one who “loves to hear” — has a wine-sipping pretension that I hate, but sometimes an ugly shoe fits. Like similarly suffixed psychopathologies, the term suggests a state of warped dependence, of inbent sublimation. I didn’t set out to be this way, but neither have I ever sought help. My symptoms are apparent, their causes obscure. What, after all, do we love when we love sound? “The neurophysiological disorder is characterized by a severe aversion to sound—and the struggle to convince others of the severity of that aversion.” “How techno’s most vaunted architect is still building sonic futures.” “A physics expert and audio engineer may have solved the mystery of a strange noise that rattled an Oregon town in 2016.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/09/24/audiophile-new-york-international-audio-show/

The visa restrictions are the latest escalation in a bigger fight over how South Africa’s white minority is represented. A far-right narrative long promoted by Afrikaner interest groups and their American allies is now helping shape U.S. policy toward South Africa: that murders in rural farming communities amount to an organized campaign against white people — rather than one part of the national crime crisis in a country with one of the highest murder rates in the world. Trump has falsely claimed that a “white genocide” is taking place in South Africa. Critics, including Rasool, argue that the Trump administration is using a narrative of white victimhood to advance its own politics at home. Washington is no longer merely amplifying that narrative. It is using it to justify preferential refuge for Afrikaners and threaten visa restrictions against people it holds responsible for policies it describes as discriminatory. The visa restrictions, announced by Rubio on Sept. 15, target “foreign nationals who are responsible for, or complicit in, the enactment or implementation of laws or policies that enable uncompensated land seizures, race-based discrimination, and/or the incitement of imminent violence against members of minority ethnic or racial groups in South Africa.” The State Department announcement says certain family members could also be affected, without providing further detail. * Britain’s last great airship
  Aeon | 24 Sep 10:01 UTC

  FULL TEXT: R.101 airship parked in Cardington, UK. Image by the Royal Aeronautical Society In this short video, Bill Hammack, an engineer and professor at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign, details the extraordinary making and tragic ending of the British airship known as R101. Conceived in 1924 and first flown in 1929, R101 was intended to fly a regular route from England to India. Hammack uses archival images and his own deep research to trace the engineering and history of this remarkable vessel, which the British government planned as the flagship of a fleet that would criss-cross its then-sprawling empire. Those ambitious plans were abandoned after the airship crashed in France in October 1930, killing 48 of the 54 people on board. Hammack offers a riveting peek into a brief moment in aviation history – one ended for good by the crash of the Hindenburg in 1937 – when it appeared as if commercial airships might become a regular sight in skies across the world. The video accompanies Hammack’s book Fatal Flight: The True Story of Britain’s Last Great Airship (2017), which is available as a free audiobook at his website . * China is excelling in health tech. That’s good news for the world
  Rest of World | 24 Sep 10:00 UTC
  A visitor experiences an artificial intelligence health screening system at the 2026 Ningxia Elderly Care Service Expo on August 28, 2026 in Yinchuan, China.
  FULL TEXT: If you want to see a doctor in China, you’d first go to a mobile app. You can book an appointment at a hospital using the messaging app WeChat or payment app Alipay. You can also speak with a doctor on a telemedicine platform, or chat with the AI avatar of a doctor about everything from insomnia to diabetes. The Chinese government has encouraged the expansion of digital healthcare services to provide care to the country’s 1.4 billion people, and address longstanding challenges such as an urban-rural gap in healthcare and a lagging primary care network. With a strong biotech talent pool and an efficient clinical test system, China’s drug industry is having a breakthrough moment, as well. More clinical trials are being conducted in China than in the U.S., and China now accounts for half of global drug licensing deals, as Western pharmaceutical companies purchase rights to new medicines developed in China. Globally, the rise of China’s prowess in health tech has been met with both awe and suspicion. Ruby Wang, a physician and consultant who has worked in health tech in China and across the world, writes about these tensions in her upcoming book China Cure: The Rise of a Biotech, AI Medicine, and Global Health Superpower . Wang spoke to Rest of World about China’s health innovation and what it means for the world. With a massive population, you have to prioritize convenience and access.” The interview has been edited for length and clarity. The Chinese population is very optimistic about AI, and one of China’s most popular AI apps is a health app . How does Chinese society view AI in healthcare, and how is it different from the West? In the U.K. and in the U.S., tech is always an add-on in the health system. You are trying to screw an extra bit onto a rusty machine, whereas in China, because the infrastructure and the digital rails are so technologically enabled, society accepts that as normal. With a massive population, you have to prioritize convenience and access because there’s no other way to meet your needs. It’s not only China. I’ve seen it in Brazil, Kenya, and Ethiopia; when you’re a country that’s actively, frantically developing, you don’t have the luxury of waiting and watching, you just have to act.   FULL TEXT: America prides itself on religious freedom, a principle that atheist Nichole Manning passionately believes Texas refuses to extend to her family. The 5th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals found the law constitutional in April, so it remains in effect. A petition to review the case is pending before the U.S. Supreme Court. But if justices weigh history as heavily as they have in other major cases, the historical record actually gives the plaintiffs a strong argument of their own. Behind the constitutional dispute lies an older question. How should public schools handle religion? Americans have argued for centuries over what role, if any, religion should play in public education and whose religion public schools should emphasize. Whatever the Supreme Court eventually decides about the Ten Commandments, that debate will continue. Existing Supreme Court precedent about Ten Commandments displays is muddled. For many years, the Supreme Court relied on the framework from Lemon v. Kurtzman to decide whether a state had violated the First Amendment’s prohibition on laws “respecting an establishment of religion.” That framework famously focused on avoiding an “excessive government entanglement with religion.” Applying this test, the U.S. Supreme Court found it unconstitutional to use public money to pay for certain expenses at private religious schools, including supplements to teachers’ salaries. In 1980, the Supreme Court used the Lemon v. Kurtzman framework to evaluate a Kentucky law mandating that every public school classroom post the Ten Commandments. In a short opinion, the court concluded that the law violated the First Amendment’s Establishment Clause because the “pre-eminent purpose for posting the Ten Commandments on schoolroom walls is plainly religious in nature.” It acknowledged that public schools could include study of the Ten Commandments and other portions of the Bible as part of a curriculum to help students better understand history or literature. Narrative writing sets down details in an order that evokes the writer’s experience for the reader, she announced. This seemed obvious but also radical—no one had ever said it so plainly to us. * Water Scarcity in Jordan Is Causing an Agricultural Crisis

  In Jordan's Azraq oasis, farmers are struggling to survive as climate change and decades of groundwater depletion ruin their prospects. The post Water Scarcity in Jordan Is Causing an Agricultural Crisis appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: In Jordan, people remember retreating shorelines. From the oasis in Azraq to the Dead Sea in the Jordan Valley, bodies of water have been shrinking, leaving behind cratered land and shriveled shrubs in their wake. When asked about the former glory of their country’s wetland areas, residents are gripped by a powerful wistfulness. In the town of Azraq, 67 miles from the capital city of Amman, everyone who was born before the 1990s has a story to tell about the oasis that once was. Naif Alafeer used to wrangle large fish with his friends, Fadi Esaid used to cup his hands in the water and drink from them after hours of playing in the sun, and Shaira Alafeer used to dig with her fingers just beneath the surface to access the water that lurked there. Azraq — which means blue in Arabic — was the perfect moniker for the freshwater pools and springs that lapped at one’s feet. In the early 20th century, when residents initially settled in the area, they bought arable land because the brimming streams promised them perpetually irrigated farms and bountiful harvests. But I saw a vast, unending landscape before me. The ground cracked underfoot, a reminder of why Jordan ranks among the world’s most water-poor nations. Azraq’s story is a microcosm of the larger tragedy unfolding in the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan. Annual precipitation in the kingdom varies regionally between roughly 2 and 7.5 inches. Each person in Jordan is projected to have access to only 16,000 gallons of potable water annually. By comparison, 2024 data suggests that New York City’s 8.4 million residents had a per capita consumption of 118.19 gallons per person daily, which puts their annual consumption at roughly 43,139 gallons. Jordan relies on a public water system that supplies allotments of water once or twice a week to individual households. As a result, rooftop water tanks have become a permanent fixture of Jordan’s cityscapes. “A single distribution zone gets around 36 hours of water supply per week, but in the north, there are only six hours of water supply per week,” said Christian Klassert, a research associate who consults with Stanford University’s Jordan Water Project. While the country requires 1.4 billion cubic meters of water annually, it was already running a deficit of about 500 million cubic meters as of 2023.   https://newlinesmag.com/reportage/water-scarcity-in-jordan-is-causing-an-agricultural-crisis/

* Still On Its Way to Cleveland
  Longreads | 22 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Nine months, 25,000 miles, one phone cord, and the kindness of strangers.
  FULL TEXT: Longreads has published hundreds of original stories—personal essays, reported features, reading lists, and more—and more than 14,000 editor’s picks. Mark and Betsy Blondin | Longreads | September 22, 2026 | 2,702 words (10 minutes) We left Boyne City, Michigan, in the fall of 1996 in a 34-foot Coachmen motorhome towing a minivan, with three children, a golden retriever not originally part of the plan, and a desktop computer on a stand bolted to the floor where a chair used to be. We stopped in Flint to say goodbye to family and retrofit the motorhome for the road. Mark’s father had died that June. His mother was devastated, and for a while the question wasn’t which route to choose but whether to go at all. We left Flint on October 4. Five miles later, the motorhome shut down. A few miles after the fuel line was fixed, we were surprised by a flat tire on the van’s tow dolly, and then, unbelievably, we bent a bumper while putting the van back on the dolly. It was past seven in the evening. We were finally on our way to Cleveland, Ohio. That was day one. What we built over the following nine months—25,000 miles through 40 states—would go on to earn a Yahoo! Cool Site designation, a presentation at a US Department of Education national conference, and a place in the federal Education Resources Information Center archive. On the road, we filed over 1,500 emails and website dispatches from museum lobbies, hotel reception desks, libraries, and wherever we could find a phone jack and someone willing to let us run a telephone cord to their wall. Google would not exist for another two years. The word “blog” had not yet been coined. We were doing something that had no name and no manual, and we were doing it over a dial-up modem while living in a motorhome. * America is in the wrong AI race with China
  Rest of World | 22 Sep 10:00 UTC
  The U.S. is framing its rivalry with China as one of technical superiority, failing to recognize that public trust and robust consumer protections are necessary for long-term dominance.
  FULL TEXT: In the U.S., the “AI race” is understood as a distinctly anti-China battle, framing model performance as the deciding factor. U.S. policymakers have, across multiple administrations, shaped the country’s entire regulatory stance to be distinctly light-touch, underpinned by the belief that regulation could lose us this global battle for artificial intelligence dominance. Recently, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman admitted the AI revolution was going slower than expected, blaming “human habits and institutional inertia.” The data tells a different story: Americans increasingly distrust the technology even as AI companies race to place it at the center of everyday life. A majority of Americans are now more concerned than excited about AI, while trust in the government to regulate AI effectively remains weak . The problem is not confined to abstract anxiety about the future. China has recognized that governing AI means governing its deployment, not merely the capabilities of its models. AI is beginning to reshape electoral politics, becoming the hot-button issue for the U.S. midterm elections. Conservative voters, angry about the water, electricity, and land demands of AI infrastructure such as data centers, say they are preparing to “ break rank .” The technology industry has discovered a basic political truth: People will not support an AI future if they believe its costs are being imposed on them while its benefits accrue elsewhere. In stark contrast, what little conversation is entertained in Washington focuses on narratives of “uncontrollable rogue agents” and loss of control. Consumer protection is too often treated as the mundane cousin of AI safety. Meanwhile, lawsuits accumulate on harmful manipulation, AI toys marketed to children surface explicit content, commonly available AI models easily allow nonconsensual deepfakes, and AI introduced in online maps amplifies misinformation. Effective consumer protections give users agency: the ability to know when they are interacting with an AI system, understand when AI has generated or altered information, challenge consequential automated decisions, and seek redress when things go wrong. Absent these protections, younger “digital native” Americans are clear : They will not willingly purchase or use these products. There is an uncomfortable geopolitical irony here.   FULL TEXT: Dried caps of the mushroom Amanita muscaria . Photo by Yarphoto/Getty Images In late summer 2023, I found myself in a smoke shop looking at a package of ‘magic mushroom’ gummies. That phrase, magic mushrooms, usually calls to mind one type of mushroom: those containing psilocybin, the compound showing promise as a therapy for depression, addiction and other psychopathologies. But these gummies were not psilocybin. Instead, the packaging described Amanita muscaria , the red-capped, white-spotted toadstool of fairy-tale forests, iPhone keyboards and Super Mario. I knew a little about the folklore of Amanita muscaria . I had first seen it discussed in an episode of the show Hamilton’s Pharmacopeia (2016-21), which begins with a claim I now hear often: that Amanita muscaria may be among the oldest human intoxicants. The New York banker-turned-ethnomycologist R Gordon Wasson, whose Life magazine essay from 1957 introduced psilocybin mushrooms to a mass Western audience, certainly took this idea seriously. In his book Soma: Divine Mushroom of Immortality (1968), he argued that the Vedic soma – a ritual drink in ancient Indian religions – was made from Amanita muscaria . The mushroom has also been linked, sometimes carefully and sometimes wildly, to the origins of Santa Claus , Viking berserkers and even Christianity. Some believe its other name, ‘fly agaric’, stems from its use as a natural pesticide for house flies; the word muscaria is also derived from the Latin word musca , meaning fly. Some scholars have linked Amanita muscaria to Lewis Carroll’s mushroom scene in Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland (1865), in which the Caterpillar tells Alice that ‘one side’ of the mushroom will make her taller and the other shorter, though Carroll does not identify a species. From the 1922 edition of Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland , illustrated by Gwynedd M Hudson. Photo by Alamy But I wasn’t standing in that smoke shop holding one of these products as a folklorist or looking to lose myself in Carroll’s ‘golden afternoon’. I am a public-health scientist, and my questions were pragmatic: what exactly was in this package? Could it hurt someone? Were these products legal? The first thing I did was what I usually do. I went to the scientific literature. And down the rabbit hole I tumbled. When they disembarked from the plane, the men were in handcuffs and leg chains, and were unshackled before the group was driven to a hotel in Mengo, a hilly suburb overlooking Kampala city, he said. “Bringing them to a country like Uganda, where people are struggling to find decent work and where they are depending on handouts from people for a meal, was quite traumatic,” he said. “They had not been told what they were being taken for and had no idea where they were, and did not have means of communication and cash to spend.” Okot added that they looked gaunt and haggard. The deportation and resulting trauma were the result of an Asylum Cooperative Agreement that the Ugandan government signed with the United States in July 2025. * Tomato Cure
  Longreads | 21 Sep 14:01 UTC

  FULL TEXT: For Orion Magazine , Seth Kaplan writes of turning to his tomato plants as a way to decompress after a long day at work. In this brief but beautiful essay, he revels in the tactile pleasures of trimming leaves and pinching suckers, of tending beings that make no demands. The tomatoes do not have opinions, or so I tell myself, though it is mostly a lie. For they do lean toward the sun and then, perversely, away from it. They sulk when I overwater and they sulk when I forget, and they have firm convictions about how much attention is too much. But whatever they want, they show me plainly. A leaning plant wants turning. A pale, unhappy one wants trimming. A bushy one wants care. There is no tone in it—no door slamming or softly clicking shut upstairs, no message that says fine in a way that is clearly not fine. When a tomato is unhappy with me, it tells me in a language I can actually answer. And it does tell me. Skip a few days—a work trip, a long weekend, some obligation that drags me out of the side yard—and they turn unruly, almost insolent. “The bed of Lake Superior is part museum, part graveyard.” “Nature’s networks, fluidity, and diversity are the keys to our future.”

It began on Jan. 7, and Mokha had been declared recaptured by Jan. 23. This first stage secured roughly 50 miles of coastline, although several more weeks were required to clear Houthi pockets from the surrounding areas. The operation had taken about 16 days to reach Mokha. Almost a decade later, the same geography changed hands in roughly seven days. On Sept. 4, the Houthis launched a massive lightning offensive that swept through Hays and Khawkha and brought their forces back to Mokha and the entrance to the strait. * He was probably right
  Aeon | 21 Sep 10:00 UTC
  Cicero’s life and thought are a testament to the idea of probabilia: we should be confident in our beliefs, but never certain - by Massimo Pigliucci Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A statue of Cicero at the Capitoline Museums, Rome, Italy. Photo by Alessandra Benedetti/Corbis via Getty Images How tightly should you hold your beliefs? You face the question several times a week without noticing. Hold them too loosely and you don’t stand for anything, hold them too tightly and you become the sort of person no argument can reach. Most of us swing between the two and have no vocabulary for the ground in between. A Roman lawyer worked one out, under pressure far worse than anything we are likely to face, and it is still the best answer available. The time is early 45 BCE, the place a villa near Lucrine Lake, northwest of Naples, in southern Italy. One of the most fascinating and controversial figures in the entirety of Western history, Marcus Tullius Cicero, is finishing writing a book that will be known as the Academica , where he explains the nature of sceptical philosophy and why it is so crucial not just to the advancement of human knowledge, but to living a good life. It is a strange moment to retire into one’s villa to write. The Roman Republic, so beloved by Cicero, is about to be destroyed by aspiring autocrats: Julius Caesar, a longtime friend and now a political opponent, has declared himself dictator for life, a move that will soon lead to his assassination on the Ides of March 44 BCE. On the personal front, Cicero has recently divorced Terentia, his wife of 30 years, and a few months ago his beloved daughter Tullia died from complications following childbirth. You would think a man whose private and public worlds are collapsing so dramatically would hardly have the focus to write philosophy. Yet for Cicero such writing is a form of therapy, and the treatises he produces will influence medieval Christians, Renaissance authors, and the Founders of the American Republic. C icero managed all this because of his adopted philosophy, ‘Academic Skepticism’, so-called because it characterised, for the period from 266 to 90 BCE, the Academy originally founded by Plato. He used to be treated as a mere populariser, a gifted Roman relaying Greek ideas he did not really own, but scholars abandoned that uncharitable verdict decades ago as it became clear that Cicero argued and decided among his sources rather than simply transcribing them. He also built a Latin philosophical vocabulary from scratch, and much of the abstract terminology of European thought descends from his coinages. On the question that mattered most to his own school – wheth
  https://aeon.co/essays/a-good-sceptic-has-more-than-doubt-and-less-than-certainty

It wasn’t, in fact, revolutionary. It was terrorism — a cancer that could be cut out. Nevertheless, debates over its validity raged among both American and British intelligence services, where the narrative’s proponents would forego nuanced understanding of the motivations of plane hijackers or the Baader-Meinhof Gang in favor of grand geopolitical machinations. “They’re picking up stuff like ‘Carlos the Jackal was a KGB agent,’ which he never was — the CIA and British intelligence often said he was and pushed that into the media. It was complete rubbish, and they knew it,” Burke says. Burke is particularly interested in the rise of a secular, leftist form of political violence with its origins in the Middle East in the late 1960s. * 50 Years of Yousra
  New Lines | 18 Sep 10:46 UTC
  The actor has spent five decades crossing genres and generations, making her one of Egyptian cinema’s rare constants. The post 50 Years of Yousra appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Among the many stars Egypt has produced during its decades at the epicenter of Arab cinema, few have been such a permanent fixture as Sivin Hafez Nessim, known to the world simply as Yousra. It is now the 50th year since the celebrated performer debuted on screen in the 1977 film “Fatat Tabhath Aan al-Hubb” (“A Girl Searching for Love”), and despite her esteemed status and countless awards, the 71-year-old continues to perform. Her latest film, “El Set Lamma (Veto)” (“Ms. Lamma (Veto)”), was released in August 2026. The key to Yousra’s longevity has been her versatility, both in her acting and in her ability to adapt to an ever-changing film industry. It is perhaps fitting that Yousra’s career began with “Fatat Tabhath Aan al-Hubb,” as in some ways the film’s title could describe her own story. Born in Cairo on March 10, 1955, Yousra’s parents divorced when she was young. She was raised primarily by her father and has spoken candidly about the sadness she feels at being deprived of her mother for much of her childhood. This year, Yousra celebrates another milestone — 30 years of marriage to filmmaker Khaled Selim, son of former professional soccer player and actor Saleh Selim. The godmother of Egyptian cinema has no children of her own, a fact she has discussed openly over the years. “You know, I always dreamt of being a mother, now I thank God that I’m not,” she told Harper’s Bazaar in 2023, a sentiment that is still taboo, especially when espoused by a woman of influence. With characteristic honesty, she went on to confess that the fear of losing one’s children is part of the reason she is glad not to have any. “But I’m the ‘mother’ of all my friends’ children. And I do that with love and with pleasure,” she added. Yousra’s private life has been the source of much media speculation over the years, to which she has tended to respond with stoicism. “Every rumor resembles the person who spreads it,” she told an interviewer in 2000. Two things stand out in speaking to Yousra, her friends, her colleagues and her fans. The first is that she is a true artist, dedicated primarily to the aesthetic rather than the commercial side of her craft. * The case for a robot tax to redistribute wealth
  Rest of World | 18 Sep 10:00 UTC
  In an excerpt from his book, “Innovate for Impact: A Roadmap to Sustainable Technology Beyond AI,” Alessandro Crimi explains that a tax on automation is more effective than retraining labor.
  FULL TEXT: AI has the potential to revolutionize economies by opening up new avenues for innovation, production, education, and problem solving. It also has the potential to increase current disparities and inequalities , and exacerbate environmental issues if it is not addressed critically. Integrating AI-driven innovation with social ideals that place a high priority on shared wealth, a just society, and ecological balance is a very difficult task. Retraining programs are a necessary but insufficient response to systemic displacement. A critical warning is being sounded that automation risks displacing labor and depressing wages unless addressed with safety nets and reallocation programs. Even in medieval times, which are rarely considered times of innovation, we had some sort of policies to reallocate people. The guild system, churches, and monasteries were training centers. Innovations like the windmill, or the watermill, created a demand for millwrights, who trained assistants informally. However, there is a reason why we remember medieval times as bleak; even with the retraining of people, innovations did not translate into improved living standards for the majority. Therefore, it is worth focusing on retraining policies, as well as profit sharing , taxation, and social safety nets as potential solutions for AI’s disruptions. With AI, although potential compensatory mechanisms such as new tasks and productivity gains are promised, history cautions us that such benefits are neither automatic nor equitably shared. The conversation around AI and labor, therefore, must broaden from a singular focus on retraining displaced workers to encompassing strategies for redistributing the wealth that automation creates, and interrogating the physical substrate upon which this new economy is built. Retraining programs are a necessary but insufficient response to systemic displacement. They place the full burden of adaptation on the individual and often fail to match the velocity of change. Structural policies that transform economic relationships are required to complement them, such as safety nets, reduction of working hours or universal basic income policies. The four-day workweek policy reduces the standard working week (usually to 32 hours) without cutting pay, aiming to improve the balance between work and home, productivity and mental health. UBI is a policy in which every adult receives a regular unconditional cash payment from the state to guarantee basic economic sec
  https://restofworld.org/2026/robot-tax-ai-wealth-redistribution/?utm_source=rss&utm_medium=rss&utm_campaign=feeds

* Cosmic amnesia
  Aeon | 18 Sep 10:00 UTC
  A black hole rings like a struck bell, then settles into silence, forgetting almost everything about its history. Why? - by Richard Dyer Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Artist’s impression of GW250114, the celestial collision of two black holes, observed in gravitational waves by the US National Science Foundation LIGO. Courtesy Aurore Simonnet (SSU/EdEon)/LVK/URI/LIGO/CALTECH It is 9 am on a warm July Sunday in Cambridge, as I head towards a café in the city centre. The bells of Great St Mary’s church play the familiar melody of the Cambridge Quarters . The sound, now often associated with the chimes of Big Ben in London, was first composed here in 1793. The quarters, named for the melody at each quarter-hour, are an immediately recognisable reminder of the passage of time. Each bell produces a note: a wave with a characteristic frequency that travels through the air and reaches our ears. A hammer strikes the bell, and it oscillates, pushing air molecules back and forth. Those jostling molecules reach our ears and we hear a musical note. Then the bell settles, gives up its energy, and returns to silence. My research here at the University of Cambridge often brings me back to the ringing of a bell. I don’t study the physics of sound, but I do focus on a remarkably similar process, taking place throughout the Universe. I am interested in another object that also rings: a black hole. As I sit down with a coffee, I open my laptop to a wavy signal that gradually fades to a flat line. Two black holes have finished merging – a frenzied process of coalescence – leaving an extremely brief wiggle originating from the remnant black hole. Much like the struck bell, this leftover black hole is ringing in a process called ringdown , and it’s consistent across every merger I investigate. Ringdown allows physicists to test their theories of gravity: did Albert Einstein really have the last word? Just as an off-sounding note from a bell might indicate a design flaw, a discrepancy in the frequencies of a ringing black hole could be the first crack in our understanding of gravity. That’s the scientific rationale for studying ringdown, but what fascinates me equally is what happens next. After the black hole rings, it settles into silence. In that moment, lasting only a few milliseconds, the power and intensity of the merger are lost. From the exterior, almost all of that history suddenly becomes inaccessible, and what is left is featureless. The black hole settles into a simple, peaceful object of pure spacetime, with no trace of the violence that just happened. In some respects, the black holes forget . It seems counterintuitive that some
  https://aeon.co/essays/black-holes-ring-like-bells-what-happens-next-is-stranger
